Foreword - One
Definition
The generally eccepted definition of machine tools in the
United States - power driven machines employing a cutting
tool or finishing metal by the removal of chips and
shavings - has not been adopted by the Japanese, who in
many instances use the words "mechine tools" and "machinery"
interchangeably. It has, therefore, been difficult to determine
just what has been included in the Japanese production and
import figures, and also to distinguish between a company
producing machinery and one producing machine tools.
As many tools take their names from the operations they rerform,
the designations given below will be employed in this directory,
realizing the infinite number of combinations and var'lations
possible under each beading.
Lathes
Planers
Milling machines
Drilling machines
Boring mills
Gear generating machines
Grinders
Automatic screw machines
Lapping machines
Honing machines
Miscellaneous special machine tools

Forewcrd - Two
Historical Summary of the Development of the Machine Tool
Industry
Japan may be said to have gained rank as a world power by the
aid of weapons which she did not produce herself. While there
has been a heavy-metal industry in Japan for almost fifty years,
the first China war of 1894 and the Russian war of 1904 were
fought mainly with ships and material purchased in foreign
countries.
In 1900 the first large steel steamer was built in the Mitsu-
bishi dockyard at Nagesaki. Armor plete was first produced in
Japan in 1902, and it was not until 1906 that a capital ship
was entirely built in Japan. The Japanese heavy industry,
railways, steel mills, and shipyards had been previously
operated with foreign-built tools and equipment. Tools in
use were generally manually operated and comparatively simple.
by World War I Japan's armament industry had been developed
to such an extent that Japan was able to supply arms to her
ally, Russia. Her shipbuilding industry had also been
developed to the point that she had become one of the leading
maritime nations, although most of the goods carried were
produced in foreign countries.
0-4329 P6 bu

=== Page 8 === (confidence 0.99)
Foroword - Three
After World War I, Japanese business leaders began to produce
their own goods to take to world markets. Although rapid
progress was made, the essentials necessary for the production
of the goods offered had atill to be purchased abroad.
In the thirties the warlords began to take an interest in the
field of machine tools, hitherto relatively undeveloped.
They saw that if Japan were to achieve a position of economic
independence and successfully expand on the Asiatio continent,
she must have industrial self-sufficiency, the key to which
lay in a machine tool industry that would permit full-scale
production of war dquipment.
Previous to this time, some cf the heavy industries had built
first-quality machine tools for their own use. There had also
been some production of machine tools for marketing purposes;
with few exceptions, however, the plants were small and
inefficiently-run. Efforts had also been made to build up a
machine tool export trade.
In the early thirties, Shinohara Kikai Seisakusho tried to sell
Japanese lathes and milling mechines throughout the Far East.
Since the company could not compete with German, British and
American firms in quality and price of merchandise, the attempt
was not entirely successful. As Japan gained an increasingly
0-4229 P7 bu

=== Page 9 === (confidence 0.99)
Foreword - Four
stronger foothold in Asia, however, the condition of her export
trade improved. The volume of this business, representing
mainly trade with territories in North China and occupied
Manchuria, grew from 216,000 in 1932 to ¥4,907,000 in 1936.
Despite this increase in exports, Japen still needed to
import machine tools. From 1932 to 1936 the imports of
The military party realized that Japan stood far behind the
great industrial nations of the Western world in the output
of machine tools. After it came into full control in 1936,
specialists were sent abroad to study types of plant equipment
lacking in Japan. A large-scale program was inaugurated in
which the best and most modern machine tools were to be
imported and used as models for domestic prcduction. Emphaeis
was placed on acquisition of automatic machinery for quantity
production in the automotive field, special milling machines,
gear cutters, grinders, and especially machinery for development
of jigs and fixtures.
A five year plan for quantitative as well as qualitative
expansion of the industry mas announced in 1937 with the
following aims:

Factors Affecting Machine Tool Industry Expansion
Japan was dependent on foreign industrial nations for basic
and essential goods needed for ths sucass of her plans for
self-sufficiency. The uufavorable balance of trade constituted
a considerable handicap. The opening of hostilities in Europe
in 1939 deprived Japan of some sources of imports of machine
tools; the freezing of Japanese dollar and pound sterling
holdings abroad in l94l cut off most of the remaining sources.
The program of self-sufficiency in machine tools was based
upon free and adequate supplies of materials and components
such as:
Alloy steels, both for machine tool parts,
cutting media, and jigs and fixtures.
Iron and steel castings.
Anti-friction bearings of all sizes and
degrees of finish.
First-class abrasives for various abrading
operations.
Specialized high-grade lubricants.
Measuring tools, testing tools, gauges, and
standards of highest accuracy.
A large pool of skilled, highly-trained labor.
0-4330 Pg bu

=== Page 11 === (confidence 0.99)
Foreword - Six
The quantities of alloy steels, iron and steel castings, anti-
friction bearings and abrasives needed by the machine tool
industry represent only a amall portion of the total Japanese
available supply. The chief problem would be one of allooation
of materials. Considering the importance of the machine tool
industry to a war economy, this industry would undoubtedly
receive its essential share of these materials.
The Japanese have built up a rather Jarge machine tool accessory
establishment for manufacturing tools, jigs and fixtures, eto.
Machine tools ordered from the United States were ordered without
these accessories which apparently were to be supplied by
domestic or European firms.
The operation of machine tool facilities depends to a great
extent upon the supply of lubricants. Befcre the outbreak of
the war, Japan imported almost 95% of the high-grade oils and
greases essential to her industrial activity, Japanese require-
ments for lubricants during the wartime period have undoubtedly
increased heavily; the quality of available lubricants is
believed to have gradually and steadily deteriorated. There is
evidence of some use of lubrication substitutes derived from
vegetable oils, particularly from castor or tung oils.
Japanese manufacturers apparently possessed en adequate stock
of measuring instruments and other devices, judging by the
advances made in the heavy and light metals industries.

of the mcre precise and delicate instruments. Since the life
expectaney of such instruments is great in comparison with
other shop uipment, it may be assumed that there is nct as
yet & very grave shortage in this field.
Skilled workers for the machine tool inaustry heve been trained
in schcols at Nagoys, Tokyo, and Csake, which were established
by cvernment order in l9e9. Testing and research centers
were established for trainins; they alsc have served various
machine tool trade crganizaticns which have sent to them new
machinery for exhaustive tests befcre placing it in eervice.
Government "egulation of tie Machine Tcol Industry
The Machine Tool Industries Law* was passed on July 1l, 1938
to implement the Five Year Plan, with its production goel of
50,000 units by 1942. By the terms cf this legislaticn, all
tool manufecturers with plants containing more than 200
machines vere made sutject tc close government scrutiny and
regulation. The government was empowered to dictate the
tye and quantity of machine tool cutput, and the type of
research and experimental work. The financial structure of
For outline cf this law see Appendix ""

=== Page 13 === (confidence 0.99)
Foreword - Eight
these firms was put under the control of the government.
No firms could merge, suspend, contrect or expand their
activities without government permission.
This law provided increased duties on imported machinery,
reduced taxes, and established subsidies for stimulation of
production of special machine tools. Firms complying with
the terms of this law were legally recognized and licensed
as machine tool manufacturers.
Before 1938, there were only a few firms in Japan which properly
could qualify as machine tool manufecturers. There were about
lo00 small, poorly equipped and inefficiently operated estab-
lishments producing low-precision manually-oontrolled tools
such as engine lathes, drill presses, and shapers. The Machine
Tool Industries Law contemplated the scrapping of about 600 of
these firms and the licensing of the remainder. This provision
seems to have been carried out.
The Mitsubishi, Mitsui, Mangyo or Nissan enterprises furnished
much of the financial assistance required for the reorganization
of the machine tool industry of Japan. The actual start was
made by five firms known as the "Big Five", which were:
Tokyo Gasu Denki K.K.

Foreword - Eleven
After the "China Incident", military censorship was clamped
down on information related to the importation and production
of machine tools. Nevertheless, the Japanese Department of
Commerce and Industry officially announced in 1938 the goal
for domestic production of machine tools worth approximately
¥200 mi111on by 1942.
The figure for this goal was obtained by adding to the estimated
¥57 million, the
amount allotted by the government for purchases of machine
tools abroad in 1940. The freezing of Japanese dollar and
pound holdings abroad threw off-balance the above mentioned
calculation. It is reasonable to conclude that instead of
reaching the goal of 200,000, 000 1u 1942, the machine tool
industry scarcely maintained the production level for the
period of 1936-1939. The relative growth of the industry may
be seen from Appendix "B" at the end of this chapter which
shows the increase in production of machine tools and machinery,
and the labor employed in the machine tool industry for the
period 1936-1939, as compared with other industries.
●
Despite the rapid expansion of domestic production of machine
tools, demand remained far ahead of production before the
Japanese attack on the United States. As can be seen from
Table II, which foliows, the domestic output amounted to fifty
percent of demand for the period 1937-1941.

Foreword - Thirteen
The great increase in imports in 1938 had two main causes.
The tense international situation in Europe presaged war and
a consequent shutting-off of imports; at the same time the
China Incident had caused an increased demand for machine
tools in Japan.
While the estimated domestic production of machine tools
showed an appreciable increase in 1939 and 1940, it should
be noted that imports remained at a high level as compared
with years previous to i9z8. There is sufficient evidence
at hand to lead to the belief that machine tool imports
would have continued to increase, despite the greatly
augmented domestic production, throughout the years after
1940. The spreading of war in Furope, however, and the
freezing of Jepanese foreien assets, together with other
preventive actions of the United States and Great Britein
are responsible for the nearly complete elimination of
imports by 1941.
In November, l94l, the Induatriel Equipment Corporation was
21

was:
"to construct the equipment of the munitions
industries, industries for productivity ex-
pansion and other vital national industries
which ere required to be set up especially by
the State, and to utilize incomplete or idle

=== Page 19 === (confidence 0.99)
Foreword - Fourteen
equipment in time of war (including the case
of an incident corresponding to war)".
This statement clearly shows the importance of machine tool
equipment to Japan.
An article appearing in Keizai Yoho Sangyohen, August, 1940
indicates that Japen was not reaching her goal in mechine tool
production. It states: "... the index for machine tool output,
with 1936 as 100, increased to 137 in 1937 with the index for
the demand on the other hand estimated to have jumped to 180
in 1937, and 810(?) in 1938....The Government has been enforcing
its five year expansion plan with a view to boosting machine
tool output by 260% in 1941 over 1938."
The most needed and most difficult types of machine tools for
Japan to make in quantities are milling machines, gear
grinders, cylindrical grinders, and lathes, particularly
turret lathes. In the period immediately prior to the war,
before the freezing of her assets, Japan imported from the
United States $64,000,000 worth of these machine tools with
milling machines, lathes and grinders heading the list.
The production shortage in both machinery and machine tools
was noted by Domei which published an Economic Commentary,
on November 21, l944, pointing out that the manufacturing
of "actual articles" was the bottle-neck. The article
suggested that a company be set up for distributing
materials needed in the manufacturing of machinery by the

=== Page 20 === (confidence 0.98)
Foreword - Fifteen
five control associations - Industrial Machinery; Precision
Instruments; Electrical Machinery; Vehicle and Motor Cars;
and the Machine Tools Control Guild.
All available evidence suggests that Japan failed in reaching
her machine tool production goal of 50,000 unite by 1942, and
1t is not known if she was able to reach and maintain this
level after l942. At the time of setting the goal, Japen had
access to world markots and anticipated the uninterrupted
flow of many materials needed from abroad.
Some authorities believe that even as late as July, 1944, Japan
had not reached the level of the production of machine tools
needed for carrying on war industries. Other experts believe
that the production goal was reached in 1943-44 but that this
output was reduced in July of 1944 because of the slowing
down or cessation of expansion in all war industries with the
exception of the aircraft industry.
On July 25, 1944, Asahi Shimbun, a newspaper in Tokyo, stated
that the machine tool production plen had been adjusted by a
new law, the aim of which was to strengthen the production of
airplanes, and to convert the machine tool factories into
aircraft parts factories for the sake of the effective utili-
zation of the equipment and skill of the machine tool
factories. Priority was to be given to those machine tools

used in the construction of airplenes.

The Tapanese machine tool industry has been divided into five
groups as follows:

886
(Companies which are listed by Zenkoku Kojo

tories as machine tool producers; and companies
referred to in Prisoner of Var Interrogation
Reports but which do not appear in any of the
Tapanese directories. These two groups are
considered "doubtful" machine tool producers.)
The number of companies producing machine tools exclusively
is not large. The majcrity of corporations manufacture
meny other items of machinery, airplane parts, auto parts
end lccomotives, etc. It has, therefore, been impossible
to determine the amount of cepital invested exclusively in
machine tool manufacturing.

Appendix 
OUTLINE OF MACHINE TOOL INDUSTRIES LAW
OF 1938
1. Manufacturers who engage in manufacturing lathes,
drilling, milling and grinding machines, etc., with an equip-
ment of more than 200 machine tools are subject to this
regulation.
2. The expansion or modification of equipment of more
than 30 machines requires governmental permission, while the
following encouragements have been provided.
(a)
Income and profit taxes as well as local taxes
will not be levied upon the capital outlay for new
installation and expansion of equipment of a value
of more than 1,500,000 yen for a period of 5 years
beginning in the year of the completion of the in-
stallation or expansion work. The necessary equip-
ment will also be exempted from import duties.
(b)
The Government will pay compensation up to 60%
of the fixed capital  e deprecieted when depre-
ciation fails to reach such amount in 5 years after
the installation or expansion of equipment of a
value of more than 5 million yen.
(c)
 The company may issue debentures up to an amount
twice the paid-up capital, notwithstanding the pro-
vision in the Commercial Code.
(d)
Development subsidies will be granted for types
of machine tools srecified by the Government.
(e)
The import of machine tools will be restricted
or subject to additional duty up to 50% ad velorem
if necessary for the protection of home manufacturers.
3. Machine tool makers must adhere to the following
obligations:
(a)
Ihe organization, exvansion, transfer, shutting
down, or suspension of the enterprises are subject to
governmental permission. The amalgamation and disso-
lution of companies muet alsc be authorized by the
Government.
G-4229 P21 bu

=== Page 23 === (confidence 0.99)
Foreword - Eighteen
Appendix A
Page 2
(b)
The Government may order a change in selling
prices and terms of machine tools, when required
by the market situation.
(c)
The Government may order the expansion or im-
provement of equipment, the manufacture of specified
types of machine tools, and research on specific
problems concerning the production of machine tools.
4. A committee will be established to advose the
Ministry of Commerce and Industry concerning the effective
execution of this Law.

AMAGASAKI SEIKOSHO
I
GENERAL REMARKS
This company was founded in April, 1932. According to a 1939
report1 this company "absorbed Osaka Seihan. Originally it
wanted to absorb Amagasaki Seitetsu but the Ministry of
Commerce and Industry would not allow this since Amagasaki
Seitetsu made cast metals while neither Amagasaki Seikosho nor
Osaka Seihan did. The products of these merged companies
(Amagasaki Seikosho and Osaka Seihan) do not confllct with each
other. Amagasaki Seikosho makes the products listed below tith
the exception of heavy, medium and thin plate which is made by
Osaka Seihan. The only steel material that these two companies
do not make between them is wire material." There is Mitsubishi,
Kubota, Asano and Sumitomo capital in this company.

DAI NTHON KCKI K.K.
I
GENERAL REMARKS
On May 1, 1943, Osaka Wakayama Tekkosho (which up to that date
operated three factories) merged with Kinoshita Tekkojo end
Nishimori Tekkojo to form Dai Nihon Kcai K.K. The former President
of Osake Wakayama Tekkosho, Tanida Shunjiro, beceme the President
of the new company.
In June, 1937, Nishimori Tekkojo's capitalization was ¥2,000,000,
all paid up. Kincshita Tekkojo's capitalization, in 1940, was
¥2,000,000, 1,000,000 paid up. Osaka Wakayama Tekkosho had a
cap1tal of ¥7,000,000 in 1940 with ¥5,000,000 paid up. A11 factories
are under government supervision.

GENERAL REMARKS

a producer of ordnance but they are making the urgently needed
grinding machines as well as milling machines.
A l939 report said, "The machine factory of this company is the
former Tomioka factory of raga Dock. The company aims to meke
the highest quality goods in Japan, expecting to outstrip Ikegai.
It plans tc put about ¥10,000,000 in its machine tool section.
1940 and 1944 reports state that the authorities were "greatly
aiding" this compeny and new arms and "achine tocl plants had been

DAIREN KIKAI SEISAKUSHO
I
GENERAL REMARKS
Dairen Kikai Seisakusho was founded in May, 19l8. It has two
plants in Dairen and one plant in Muken. The old Mukden plants
were sold to South Manchuria Railway Company and a new one was
erected at Tetsunishi. Only the Mukden plant makes machine tools.
After the China Incident, in 1937, it turned to specific war pro-
duction and by 1939 airplane parts formed 20% of the total pro-
duction. It is a leading heavy industry concern of Manchuria.

EBARA SEISAKUSHO
I
GENERAL REMARKS
Ebara Seisakusho was founded in May, 1920, through the reorganization
of Inoguchi Shiki Kikai Jimusho.
According to a 1939 report, this company's change in production was
inevitable with the institution of controls of iron and steel.
Expansion plans fall into three groups: 1) moving to China; 2) estab-
lishment of subsidiary in Manchuria; 3) large scale production of
machine tools. A 40,000 tsubo site was purchased in Mukden for the
subsidiary, Ebara Hoten Seisakusho. A 38,000 tsubo site was decided
on at Kawasaki. This factory was completed at the end of 1940 and
production of machine tools began at the second period of 1941.

FUJIKOSHI KOZAI KOGYO
I
GENERAL REMARKS
Fujikosh1 Kozai Kogyo was established in December, l928 and is
the fourth ranking member cf the Japan Bearing Industry. It is
actually a leading steel and tool manufacturing company. On Nov-
omber, 18, 194l, the company was designated by the Ministry of
Commerce as one of the companies charged with increasing the out-
put of Japanese steel balls; it was likewise designated as one of
the five leading bearing makers for increasing bearing pmoduction
and as one of four steel companies charged with increasing the
output of special steels required by the bearing industry.
According to a Japanese P/W, Mi-l0l0, who had spent all his life in
the Toyowa area and who had worked in the main wcrks of Fujikoshi
Koza1 Kogyo (February to August, 1943), since the war, the Army and
Navy, apparently jointly, had taken over direct control of the com-
pany. In February, 1943 the two works, Iwase and Toyowa had 24,000
employees.
Fujikoshi Kozai Kogyo was capitalized at ¥10,000,000 in 1928, with
umitomo capital dominating. Early in l939 Fujikoshi's capital was
increased to ¥25,000,000 and in 1940 to ¥50,000,000. The company
began the expansion of its plants early in 194l and a 1941 report
stated that it "will merge soon with Kyushu Special Steel (Tokushu
Hagane or Tokusho Ko)." Before December 7, 194l, Fujikcshi Kozai
Kogyo ordered American machine tools to the amount of $573,364.36
of which 561,895.90 were actually shipped.

HITACHI SEIKI
I
GENERAL REMARKS
This company is the result of a merger in December, 194l, of
Kokusan Seiki, Hitachi Kosakuki and Shinohara Kikai Jeisakusho.
Kokusan Seiki was established in July, 1936 and ie a Hitachi sub-
sidiary. Mitsubishik Tokyo, in writing to Mitsubishi, New York, on
October 21, 1940, said Kokusan Seiki "is one of the first cless makers
of turret lathes in Japen and is one of the 17 Government licensed
makers." In 1940 Kokusan Seiki trebled its capital to the present
figure, evidently to take care of plant expansione and equipment.
Hitachi Kosakuki was established in May, l939.
Shinohara Kika1 Seisakusho was established in Cctober, 1934. In
1939 its capital was only ¥3,000,000. In that year it annexed
Showa Koseku Kikai Seisakusho about which company little is kmown
except that it had one plant in Tokyo which produced 8 ft. lathes
and milling machines. Hitachi bought the merged companies through
mediation of the Minister of Commerce and Industry.
Its estimated production in 194l was ¥25,000,000.

IKEGAI TEKKOSHO K. K.
I
GENERAL REMARKS
Ikegai Tekkosho K. K. was established in April, 1913. This is
one of the original sixteen licensed Japanese machine tool firms
authorized to undertake production by virtue of the Machine Tool
Law of 1938, According to a 1943 report a new plant was to be
built in Chiba-ken to manufacture engines for shop use. This
plant was to be completed by end of 1944. The capital of Ikegai
Tekkosho K. K. was to be increased to $ 50,000,000 for this purpose.
Ikegai Tekkosho K. K. has a subsidiary, Ikegai Jidosha, established
in l937, which manufactures diesel automobiles, diesel engines, and
fuel injection pumps. As of 1937, the subsidiary was capitalized at
¥ 15,000,000. It has three plants.

GENERAL REMARKS

reported to be the largest bridge, locomotive and boilermaker in Japan,
and is extensively engaged in the manufacturing of structure steel
work. It does, however, produce machine tools, but the exact type
of machine tool is unknown.

MITSUBISHI KOSAKU KIKAI
I
GENERAL REMARKS
On April 16, 194?, the machine tool section of Mitsubishi Denki
merged with Toyo Kikai to fcrm Mitsubishi Kosaku Kikai.
Reports in 1940 etated that Toyo Kikai was one of the leading
mechine tool manufacturers of Japan. Under the Machine Tool
Industries Law of l9?8 it was the only establishment legally
recognized as specializing in the production of 6 and 8 ft. engine
lathes. The nucleus of this company was the machine tool section
of Toyo Kohan. Mechinery is said to have been moved frcm the
Kudamatsu plant of Toyo Kohan to the ircshima plant of Toyo Kikai.
Toyo Kikai was contrclled for a time by the Manchuria Industrial
Development Corporation. In 1939, Mitsubishi, which owned the
bulk of the stock, took over the managenent. Sumitcmo and Mitsui
also had an interest in it. The machine tcol manufacturing section
cf Mitsubishi Denki at Nagoya was licensed and specialized in
turret lathes and grinders, with the benefit of many years of re-
search. In 1977, the plant employed over 2,500 men in its eightshops.
The new cemuny, Mitsubishi Kosaku Kikai K.A.,is probably the
largest machine tool company in Japan and employs between ter. and
twelve thcusand men, elthough it is belisved that not all of these
are employed on mechine tools. The Hiroshime plant, patterned after
American mechine tool plants, is designed for mass trcduction. It is
especially designed to prcduce lathes. a radic broadcast of April 20,
l945, reported that Mitsubishi Kosaku Kikei was to be merged with
Mitsubishi Jukcgyo.

MITSUI KOSAKUKI K.K.
I
GENERAL REMARKS
This company was formed by Mitsui Bussan Kaisha and Shibaura
Engineering Works. It was established for the purpose of
manufacturing various machine tools under a license purchased
from the Norton Company, Worcester, Mass., U.S.A. Manufacturing
drawings were purchased from Norton and machine tools from other
American firms were shipped to Japan.
There is conflicting evidence as to the name of the new company.
In March, 1939, documents received from Japan stated that the
company was to be known as "Toyo osaku Kikai." However, all
negotiations in this country for equipment were in the name of
"Mitsui Machine Tool Shop," and all shipments to Japen were con-
signed in the latter name.

NIIGA TA TEKKOSHO
I
GENERAL REMARKS
Niigata Tekkosho was founded in June, 1910, and underwent great
expansion prior to the war.
A 1939 report said the machine tool plant at Niigata City was
undergoing an overall expansion and that the Nagoya, Nishi Kanda-
cho and the Kashiwazaki-machi plants, which had specialized in
well-boring and oil manufacturing machinery, had begun to make
goods more immediately in demand.
Niigata Tekkosho is a licensed machine tool company, one of the
leading sixteen. Its estimated 1941 production was ¥15,000,000.

OKAMOTO KOGYO K.K.
I
GENERAL REMARKS
Okamoto Kogyo K.K. was established in March, 1919. According
to a l943 report, Mitsubishi and Sumitomo have large interests
in this company. Immediately after the China Incident, it ex-
panded tremendously. In June, 1937, the capital was increased
from ¥1,500,000 to ¥3,250,000. In August, 1938 1t doubled its
capital to ¥6,400,000 and at the same time the production was
shifted to war goods. Okamoto Kokuki Kogyo (Okamoto Aircraft
Industry) was established and in March, 194l, was taken over by
Okamoto Kogyo K.K. (Okamoto Industry), the joint capital totall-
ing ¥13,000,000. Mitsubishi and Sumitomo retained their interests.
Although it has five plants, only one is a known producer of
machine tools.

OSAKA KIKO
I
GENERAL REMARKS
Osaka Kiko was established in October, 1915. It was formerly
called Nippon Heiki Seizo K. K. and in September, 1920, it
annexed Osaka Kosaku Kaisha. The name was also changed at this
time to Osaka Kikai Kosakusho, end finally in 1938 to Osaka Kiko.
The technique and skill of the company is fairly well recognized.
The production of machine tools was started in 1935 on a small
scale. During 1940-1941 the company abandoned its textile machinery
works and re-equipped and expanded its Inagawa Kojo at a cost of
¥20,000,000 to make machine tools, munitions and electrical machines.
The president of this company is Mr. S. Hara.

OSAKA SEISHA ZOKI K.K.
I
GENERAL REMARKS
Osaka Seishe Zoki was established in August, 1904 by Mr. Terade
as his own enterprise. In September, l9l6, it was formed into a
Joint Stock Scmpany with a capital of ¥1,O00,O00. The capital was
1ncreased to ¥3,000,000 in 1918. At the end of January, 1935, the
name was changed to its present one.
This ccmpany was principally a chain manufacturer but became a
licensed machine tool firm in l939. In that year, it purchased
Hirao Tekkosho, which alsc made machine tools. Its capitalizetion
in June, 1938, had increased to ¥17,000,000 from ¥7,000,000 in
July, l936. By the end of August, 1943, its re-organizat1on and
expansicn program was completed.
There is evidence of some connection between Mizogouchi Gear Worke
(one of the most important gear manufacturing concerns) and Osaka
Seisha Zoki K.. Several orders for tools from the United tates
give the address of the Mizogouchi Gear Works in Osake and it
appoars to be the seme as that of Osaka Seisha Zoki K.K., Teukuda-
cho, Nishi Yodogama-ku. Mr. Mizogouchi, owner of the Mizogouchi
rear Works, is a member of the Board cf Directors of Osaka Seisha

=== Page 116 === (confidence 0.99)
-90-
RIKEN KOSAKU KIKAI
I
GENERAL REMARKS
Riken Kosaku Kikai was established in April, 1938. The president
is Baron Okochi, Doctor of Technology at Tokyo Imperial University.
This concern acquired a good reputation chiefly as a mamufacturer
of pistons, but about l940 began manufacturing machine tools and
precision testing machines, both optical and electrical. All of
these products had a very poor reputation in the market. This was
due to the labor-management theories of Baron Okochi. The Baron's
policy was to arrange his machinery so that workers needed to exercise
a minimum of skill, and a maximum of unskilled people could be em-
ployed. He also pursued the policy of establishing factories in
rural districts and making use of village workers. These problems

TOYODA KOKI
I
GENERAL REMARKS
Toyoda Koki was founded in May, 1941. According to a 1945
report the construction of forging and casting plants was
not completed. An article published in Deiyamondo, 21
December 1944, stated that the capital of this compeny is
20,000,000 of which ¥14,000,000 has been paid up. Kaisha
Shikiho, l943, gives the capital, shares and shareholders
as below.
This compeny was a subordinate machine tool company of Toyoda
Jidosha before becoming independent. Machine tools produced
by this company are indispensable for the production of pre-
cision spare parts for aircraft end automobiles. Its collec-
tion of payment for stock at 12.50 per share in June, 1944,
according to Daiyamondo, totalled ¥3,000,000 and was to be used
for expansion at a time when most machine tool companies are
being forced to shift to other industries. In the third period
of 1943, Toyoda Koki paid a 5% dividend. Profit for September,
1944 allowed a raise of dividends by 1%.

=== Page 161 === (confidence 0.98)
-135-
FUJII SEISAKUSHO
I
GENERAL REMARKS
Fujii Seisakusho was established in January, 1938, according to
the testimony of & Prisoner of War who had been salesman and
business agent for Japanese machine tool firms, "YAMAAkE invested
in this firm and managed it from the spring of l940 until the
spring of 1942, when FUJII failed and was taken over by MITSUBISHI.
FUJII was mamufacturing chiefly lathes, both large and small,
especially vertical types. It also made gun-barrel turning lathes,
beginning about April 194l. These were so long that not mre than
one could be loaded on a railroad flat-car. One such lathe was de-
livered to the OsakA Arsenal after a considerable delay, but it
failed its tests. The lack of proper engineering supervision over
the manufacture of these lathes contributed to the failure of
FUJII. About 1939, FUJII had imported a huge press, of more than
100 tons, from America through the Herbert Machine Tool Company
Ltd., Japan. It was supposed to be the largest press in Japan, and
was a moulding press for the manufacture of motor car bodies and
similar articles. FUJII was unable to make use of it, and kept it
idle. This bad venture also contributed to their failure."
II
CAPITALIZATION
Dato
Authorised
Paid-up
1940
￥1,200,000

KOKU YAKIN KIOU KAISHA
I
GENERAL REMARKS
The Physical and Chemical Research Institute, which has been
planning for some time to set up a specialized metallurgical
implement company, has established the eronautical Metallur-
gical Implement Company. By mobilizing the institute's tool
research laboratory and machine tool department, this new
cospany is expected to establish a technical department with-
in its organization.
An unofficial decision has been made to increase the new
company's capital by Y5,000,000. The executives are: Direc-
tor and President - Toshinobu Takedo; Managing- Director -
Ryuuta Komine, of the Physical and Chemical desearch Institute

=== Page 365 === (confidence 0.94)
-399-
DAIDO DIESNKI SEIKO K.K.
I
ORIERAL REMARS
Daido Denki Seiko K.K. or Daido Seiko is one of the three big alloy
steel companies in Japan -- the other two being the Yasuki Steel
Works of Hitachi Seisaku and Nippon Tokushuko. Daido Denki Seiko
mamufactures all kinds of cast and forged steel products; steel
for use in automobiles, aeroplanes, arms, and mmchine tools;
springs; electric furnaces; electrical supplies; menganese steel
rails, eto. In 1938 they started to make some mechine tools
(lathes or drilling machines) but the reception of these tools
was poor.
In January 1937 Daido Denki Seiko organized Daido Kikai Seisakusho
to manufacture machine tools and parts, metal welders, Machine
equipment was not up to expectation so that production was less
than anticipated.
II
SOURCE
Report on Daido Denki Seiko K.K., November 19, 194?, by Morris
Caldenk FEA, New York, N.Y., DSS #634.

machine tool companies in the Japanese Empire, the fol-

contains only those plants which, according to available
evidence, produce mechine tools.

appear in this list due to the absence of information
concerning their amufactured products.

PART I
SUMMARY
Japan's prewar commercial shipping tonnage during the
ten-year period 1931-1940 ranged from a low of about 5.57
million gross tons (end of l934) to approximately 7.84
million gross tons (autumn of l940). The ten-year average
and the total at the end of 1937 was about 6.3 million gross
tons.
In the matter of vessel employment, about 2.3 million
gross tons (36.5% of Japan's total tonnage) is estimated to
have been employed in overseas service at the end of l937.
Another 3.2 million g.t. (50.3% of the total) 1s estimated
to have been engaged within the limits of the "nearseas" area
(which is almost identical to the Greater East Asia area) of
which some 116 mill1on g.t. (18.4% of total) 1s estimated to
have been employed in the officlally defined "coastal seas"
and "calm water" areas. The estimate for vessels engaged in
fishing 1s about 689,000 g.t. (10.9%), and for layups about
93,000 g.t. (1.5%), 1eav1ng some 48,500 g.t. (0.8%) the
disposition of which is undetermined.
It is estimated that around l05 million long tons, or
approximately 96% of the total quantity of cargoes entering
and clearing ports in Japan Proper, were transported during
1934 by Japanese ships. An additional unknown quantity of
goods was oarried by Japanese ships on runs wholly outside
Japan Proper. of the known total, about 86.4 million tons of
cargoes are estimated to have been carried between ports in
Japan, about 7.4 million tons between Japan and the colonies
(Korea, Formosa, Karafuto, and the Mandated Islands), and
around ll million tons between Japan and foreign countries
(including Manchuria and Kwantung Provinces). No direot
comparison can be made between cargo statistics and vessel
employment figures (except between respective totals), for the
reason that the latter are compiled on a geographical basis,
while the former are compiled in accordance with political
boundaries.
In the course of the present war the average tonnage of
Japan's merchant ships has declined markedly, an inoreasing
reliance upon very small vessels has been made necessary, and
the average speed both for the merohant fleet as a whole and
for the larger ships still afloat has been reduced. A fairly

capacity of various kinds and tonnage olasses of ships on

shipping could transport annually between B1 and 65 million
long tons. Additional cargoes could be carried by barges and
certain other categories of small craft, for whioh estimates
of total tonnage afloat are not available at this time.

Part II
DISCUSSION
Size of Japanese Pre-war Commercial Fleet.
Japan's commeroial shipping fleet during the ten-year
period 1931-40 ranged in size from a low of 59,515 vessels
aggregating about 5.57 million gross tons (end of 1934) to
the level of more than 68,300 vessels aggregating something
over 7.84 million gross tons (autumn of 1940). The large
increase (over 1.5 million gross tons) that ccourred between
the end of 1936 and the end of 1939 1s attributed in part to
the admission to Japanese registry of so-called "Unusual
Imported Vessels" previously registered in China, provided for
by special legislative action. 1
Employment of Japan's Commercial Ships in Years Preceding
Pacifio War.
About 36.5% of the total shipping tonnage is estimated
to have been employed in overseas service at the end of 1957,
the period for which statistics of vessel employment are most
nearly adequate. Another 50.3% is estimated to have been
engaged within nearseas area limits (which correspond rather
olosely with the Greater East Asia sphere), of whioh 18.4% of
the total shipping tonnage 1s estimated to have been employed
in the more localized "coastal seas" and "calm water" shipping
areas. The estimate for fishing vessel tonnage is 10.9% of
the total, and that for layups and repairs 1.5%, leaving 0.8%
the disposition of which is undetermined. If the foregoing
percentages are valid for other years in the 193l-40 span, the
shipping tonnage operating in coastal and calm water areas
ranged between about 1,025,600 and 1,442,800 gross tons, while
vessels employed in the nearseas (per se) aggregated between
1,778,100 and 2,501,400 gross tona, and those in the overseas
service ranged from about 2,034,500 to 2,862,100 gross tons.
Tonnage of Cargoes Carried in Japan's Foreign, Colonial, and
Domestic Trade
The approximate total quantity of cargoes moving into and
out of the ports of Japan Proper was l09.5 million long tons
in l934, the year for which available data are most nearly
adequate. Cargoes moving between Japan and foreign countries
(including Manchuria and Kwantung Province) totalled about
15.8 million tons, those between Japan and Korea aggregated
1 Kaiun (Shipping), July 1940.

about 4.2 million tons, and goods shipped between Japan and
Formosa amounted to about 2.2 million tons. Totals for
cargoes carried between Japan and Karafuto (southern Saghalien)
and between Japan and Nanyo (Mandated Islands) are estimated
to have been about 705,000 long tons and 244,000 long tons,
respectively. The estimate for waterborne freight tonnages
between ports of Japan Proper is 86.4 million long tons. For
the mame year, railways in Japan Proper (government lines and
privately-owned lines) moved a total of io2,1 million long tons.
It 1s further estimated that about l04.8 million long tons,
or approximately 96% of the total quantity of cargoes entering
and olearing ports of Japan Proper in l934, were carried by
Japanese ships. In addition, an unknown quantity of cargoes
moving along regular and irregular routes wholly outside Japan
Proper were carried by Japanese vessels.
Significant Wartime Changes in Japan's Merchant Shipping.
Among the major changes that have occurred in the
composition of Japan's merohant fleet since the beginning of
the Pacifio War are the following:
1. Deoline in average tonnage of freight ships.
Aocompanying the very great decrease in total Japanese
merohant ship tonnage brought about by the exoess of
losses over new construction and acquisitions, has
been a steady decline in average tonnage of ships
still in existence. Losses have been heaviest among
ships in the high and medium tonnage categories,
whioh have generally been used for the longer and more
hazardous voyages. Moreover, the construction of
new vessels during the war has become conoentrated on
standard types of both wooden and steel ships in the
medium and amaller tonnage blocks, and particular
emphasis has been placed on the mass-production of
"E" type ore carriers of between 800 and 900 gross tons.
As of July 1, 1945 the average tonnage of ships still
afloat was around 700 gross tons, compared with an
average of about 1,150 g.t. for the end of 1935 and
about 1,250 g.t. for the end of 1939. 1
1
Averages are for units of l00 gross tons and over. Estimate
for July 1, l945 excludes auxiliary-powered vessels of
100-499 g.t. See Table 4b. Append1x A:

=== Page 579 === (confidence 0.98)

2.
Reduction in speed of vessels. The important factors
that have contributed to a general slowing down of
the Japanese merohant marine, excluding the purely
navigational factors, are:
(a) the loss of many of the fastest units during
the war,
(b)
the comparatively slow speeds of the standard
types of ships which make up practically l00%
of newly constructed tonnage, and
(c)
the incidence of fouled bottoms and the lack of
adequate maintenance repairs resulting from
the acute wartime strain on repair and re-
conditioning facilities.
3.
Increased use of small vessels and towed barges.
To a far greater extent than before the war Japan has
been forced to rely upon the use of small wooden
ships and upon barges and other engineless oraft,
towed by tugs, particularly for movements through
the Inland Sea of Kyushu coal to industrial centers in
Honshu. It is probable, also, that many small wooden
craft are by now being used to transport Hokkaido
coal to ports in northern Honshu, for transshipment
by rail to Honshu industrial consumers. Before the
war, suoh shipments were waterborne all the way from
Hokkaido to the industrial centers, generally in
medium-sized steel vessels.
The aweeping changes in the employment of Japan's
merchant shipping that have been effected during the
course of the war are based on the central faot that
ship disposition in wartime is governed primarily
by the operational and supply needs of the armed
forces and war industry. since the beginning of the
war Japanese merchant ships have operated almost
entirely within the "Greater East Asia" Zone, or
roughly within the officially defined Nearseas Area,
where only about one-half of Japan's total tonnage
is estimated to have been engaged around the end of
1937. 1 An important share of one transport job
performed by Japan's coastal shipping before the
war, - that of moving Kyushu coal to industrial users
in Honshu - was shifted to the railroads with the
completion of the Kwanmon tunnel which connects
Kyushu and Honshu.
1 See Table 5a, Appendix A.

Considerations Rolating to Japanese Shipping After Hostilities.
A determination as to what merohant ship tonnage the
Japanese should be allowed to have after hostilities have
ceased needs to be based upon the resolution of suoh fundamental
questions as the following:
1. (a) What kinds and quantities of commodities will
Japan be allowed to import from and/or export
to areas outside the home islands; and
(b)
what portion, if any, of allowed imports
and/or exports will the Japanese be permitted
to transport in Japanese ships?
2. (a)
What type and degree of control over trans-
portation within the limits of Japan's home
islands will be undertaken by the Allies?
(b)
If a high degree of control is assumed, what
kinds and quantities of commodities will be
allowed to move?
(0)
What level of utilization of the railroad
system shall be selected, what restrictions
on capacity will war damages impose at least
temporarily, and what share of total permitted
cargo movements will be assigned to the rail-
ways?
3.
What kinds and amount of vessels will the Japanese
be allowed to have for purposes other than freight-
carriage, such as fishing, harbor control, dredging,
ice breaking, and ferry service (including train
ferries)? The total number of fishing craft (units
of 5 gross tons or over) ranged from 23,378 in 1933
to 25,421 in 1937, and aggregate gross tonnage for
1937 is evaluated to have been 688,700. The aggregate
tonnage of Japan's vessels construoted by 1937 and
listed as dredgers, 1ce breakers, ferries, harbor
control vessels, etc., was about 77,700 gross tons. 1
When the foregoing questions are resolved and an approxi-
mate total volume of waterborne cargo movements is thus arrived
at, a solid basis will have been established for determining
what kinds and tonnage of merchant mhips Japan shall be allowed
to have following the end of hostilities.
M.E.W., Ships Available To The Enemy In Far Eastern Waters.
July 1944. The total 1s complete for units 1,000 g.t. and
over, but not necessarily complete as regards those under
1,000 g.t. Included are 21 dredgers (13,200 g.t.), 10 10e
breakers (6.900 g.t.), 25 ferries (42.200 g.t.) and 46 others
(tugs, cable shipa, eto.; 15,400 g.t.).

Latest estimates of the shipping Japan has afloat give,
for vessels l00 g.t. upwards, a total of 3,175 units aggregat-
1ng 2,309,000 gross tons, of which 2,540 units tota1ling
1,849,000 g.t. are estimated as serviceable. 1/ The correspond-
1ng totals for ships 1,000 g.t. and over are 465 un1ts aggregat-
1ng 1,534,000 g.t. (afloat) and 372 units totalling 1,227,200
g.t. (serviceabie).
Other calculations have been made showing the annual bulk
cargo carrying capacity on two representative runs within the
limits of Japan's home islands of given tonnages of several
kinds of vessels. 2 On the basis of such calculations, the
shipping Japan 1s estimated to have possessed on July 1, 1945
could carry:
(a) 50.7 million metric tons of bulk cargoes (other
than coal) annually, plus whatever freight might
be moved on return voyages (see below), on a
typical Inland Sea run between the Moji area and
the Kobe/Osaka area (480 nautical miles round trip); or
(b)
64.6 million metric tons of miscellaneous bulk cargoes
(plus other freight on return trips) annually on a
typical north Japan run between Hokkaido and Honshu
(600 miles round trip).
Additional tonnages could be transported by barges, by
various vessels (steam, motor and sail) under loo gross tons
each and by auxiliary-powered units over l0o g.t., for which
reliable, satisfactory estimates of the number and tonnage still
afloat are not available at present. However, Japan 1s known
to be using numerous auxiliary-powered small craft, 3/ and may
be assumed to possess a large number of vessels in the other
categories mentioned. Calculations of the bulk carrying capacity
of such small craft are, therefore, included in Table 7,
Appendix A. 4/

It is possible that whatever policy and program of merchant
shipping controls may be developed for the immediate post-
hostilities period would favor the retention and/or build-
ing of the smaller kinds of vessels, for several reasons.
That is, because of ease of construction, suitability for
home island commerce and unsuitability for long-distance
navigation or surreptitious training of naval crews, and
the probable surplus of manpower, there might be some merit
in the general proposition of having Japan's post-hostilities
commercial ship tonnage "ceiling" (if any) composed of a
0-6264 Po
large proportion of small craft.

Each 100,000 gross tons of serviceable ships 1,000 g.t.
and over could transport 4,200,000 m.t. of coa1 or 2,884,000
m.t. of other bulk cargoes annually on the typical Inland
Sea run, or could carry 3,696,000 m.t. of miscellaneous
bulk cargoes anmually on the North Japan run.
For ships less than 1,000 g.t., the corresponding annual
capacities for each allocation of l0o,000 g.t. averages about
3,560,000 m.t. of coal or 2,467,500 m.t. of other bulk cargoes
on the Inland Sea route, and about 3,092,500 m.t. of miscellaneous
bulk freight on the northern route. 1
In all cases, the foregoing basic, or "one way", cargo
carrying capacities could theoretically be Inoreased somewhat,
depending upon how olose traffic approaches a perfect balance
on partioular:runs. In Japan the general tendency has been for
bulky commodities to move from Kyushu and Hokkaido to manufactur-
ing centers in Honshu, and for finished goods to move in the
reverse directions. It is caloulated that maximm or "round
trip" capacity figures would probably be little more than, or
not over llo% of, basic capacity computations. 2
1

of motive power, while calculations of carrying capacity
vary as between sailing vessels, full-powered steam and
motor, and auxiliary-powered vessels. Practically all
sailing and auxiliary-powered vessels are less than 1,000
g.t. each.

OFFICIAL DEFINITION
F JAPANESE NAVIGATION AREAS 1/
There are four navigation areas or zones, for Japanese
shipping, as follcwss
1. Calm water area, which includes lakes, river mouths,
and the waters within harbor limits;
2. Coastal seas area, which includes eight sub-zones:
(a) Waters extending 20 nautical miles from the
coastal lines of
Hokkaido and off-lying islands;
(2)
Honshu and off-lying 1slands;
(3)
Shikoku;
(4)
Kyushu and off-lying islands;
(5)
Formosa, Boko Retto, Taihokushu Panchiayu,
Tai Tung Ting, Hoshaotao, and Hungtonyu;
(b)
Waters within the line from Chiba-ken, Anbo-gun,
Noshime bank to Shizuoka-ken, Kamo-gun, Fumurosaki
through Tokyo-fu, Kamitsu Jima;
(c)
Waters within the line from Akita-ken, Yuri-gun,
Ennetsubi to Ishikawa-ken, Hoshi-gun, Saruyama
bank through Ishikawa-ken, Hesakura Shima;
(a)
Waters within the lines from Yamiguchi-ken,
Toyoura-gun, Kannonsaki to southern province of
Keisho Utsusaki, and from Nagasaki-ken, Kitamatsuura-
gun, northern tip of Seiketsu-to to southern
province of Zenra Kodotsuzan peninsula, southeast
tip;
(0)
Waters within the lines from Hokkaido, Soya-gun,
Nosamusaki to Karafuto, Nishi-notorosaki, and
from Hokkaido, Soya-gun, Soyasaki to Karafuto,
Chujisosaki;
(f)
Waters extending 20autical miles from the coast
of Chichi Jima and Haha Jima; Tokyo-fu;
(g)
Waters extending 20 nautical miles from the coastal
line of Anami Gunto, Kagoshima-ken;
(b)
Waters extending 20 nautical miles from the coastal
line of Okinawa Shima and various other islands
of Shimajiri-gun, Okinawa-ken.

SECRET
Appendix C
NOTES REGARDING
JAPANESE MERCHANT SHIPPING STATISTICS
General classifications of merohant ship employment are
as follows: 1
A. Basic Types of Service.
1. "Fixed period" (or regular) marine routes, for
which the operating ccmpany Is called a "liner".
(a) Ordered marine routes (mervices ordered by à
governmental aithority): subsidies are paid
by the Government, in return for which variems
obligations are imposed as to type, speed,
and age of sjips, frequency of sailing, and
the carrying of mail;
(b) Free marine routes.
2.
a "tramper".
Ownership.
1. "Shasen", or ships owned by major companies,
1ncluding: Nippon Yusen, Osaka Shosen, Xinkai
Yasen, Chosen Yusen, Nanyo Kaiun, Kita Mippon
Kisen, Nisshin Kisen.
2. "Shagaisen", or ships owned by other than major
companies.
There is no clear distinotion between "shasen" and
"shagaisen". Formerly, "shasen" meant ships employed
in ordered marine routes, and "shagaisen" were those
employed in free marine routes and in tramp shipping.
This distinction became blurred, however, because some
"shagaisen" were employed in fixed marine routes. An
approximate distinotion is that "shasen" are those
ships employed in fixed routes (with primary emphasis
on passenger service) and "shagaisen" are those engaged
in unfixed routes (with primary emphasis en freight
service).
1
Koisai Kiji No Kiso Chishiki (Basic Knowledge on Eeonomie
Articles), 1938.
